			&\le e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon {\le} T+1} \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!} \frac{{\Absolute*{\varepsilon(n+1) - (t-s)}^\xi}}{\varepsilon^\beta (n+1)^\beta} \nonumber\\
			&\qquad +e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon {\le} T+1} \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!}\frac{{\Absolute*{\varepsilon(n+1) - (t-s)}^\xi}}{(t-s)^\beta}\nonumber\\
			&:=J_{1,T}(t-s,\varepsilon) + J_{2,T}(t-s,\varepsilon),\label{ineq:j1j2 composition approx poisson}
		\end{align}
		
		For each $t,\,\varepsilon>0$, let $N_\varepsilon(t)\sim {\operatorname{Pois}}(t/\varepsilon)$. Using Jensen's inequality and the fact that $\xi \in (0,1]$, we see for each $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ and $t>s \ge 0$,
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:j2 decomposition poisson moments}
			\begin{aligned}
				J_{2,T}(t-s,\varepsilon)&\le \frac{{\mathbb E}\left[ \Absolute*{{\varepsilon\bigl(N_\varepsilon(t-s)+1\bigr)} -(t-s) }^\xi\mathbf{1}_{\{{\varepsilon N_\varepsilon(t-s)\le T+1}\}}\right]}{(t-s)^\beta}\\
				&\le \frac{{\mathbb E}\left[ \Absolute*{{\varepsilon\bigl(N_\varepsilon(t-s)+1\bigr)} -(t-s) }^\xi\right]}{(t-s)^\beta}\\
				&\le \frac{{\mathbb E}\left[ \Absolute*{{\varepsilon\bigl(N_\varepsilon(t-s)+1\bigr)} -(t-s) }^2\right]^\frac{\xi}{2}}{(t-s)^\beta}\\
				&\le \frac{\Parentheses*{2\varepsilon(t-s) +\varepsilon^2}^\frac{\xi}{2}}{(t-s)^\beta},
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		where we used the variance of the Poisson distribution in the last line.
		
		For the first term, let $p \ge {2}$ be a sufficiently large {even integer} so that $\beta \frac{p}{p-1}<1$, then by H\"{o}lder's inequality, we see
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:j1 decomposition approx poisson}
			\begin{aligned}
				J_{1,T}(t-s,\varepsilon) & \le \left(e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon {\le} T+1} \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!}  {\Absolute*{\varepsilon(n+1) - (t-s)}^{\xi p}}\right)^\frac{1}{p}\\
				&\qquad \times\left( e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon {\le} T+1} \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!} \left(\varepsilon(n+1) \right)^{-\beta \frac{p}{p-1}} \right)^\frac{p-1}{p}
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		Since $\xi \in (0,1]$, we see by Jensen's inequality and  Lemma \ref{lem:poisson moment bounds} that the first term is bounded by
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:j1 decomposition poisson moments}
			\begin{aligned}
				\mathbb E\left[ \Absolute*{{\varepsilon\bigl(N_\varepsilon(t-s)+1\bigr)} - (t-s)}^{p\xi} \right]^\frac{1}{p}&\le \varepsilon^\xi{\mathbb E}\left[\Absolute*{N_\varepsilon(t-s) + 1 - \frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}}^p \right]^\frac{\xi}{p}\\
				&\lesssim \varepsilon^\xi +\varepsilon^\xi {\mathbb E}\left[\Absolute*{N_\varepsilon(t-s)  - \frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}}^p \right]^\frac{\xi}{p} \\
				&\lesssim \varepsilon^\xi \Parentheses*{1+ \Parentheses*{\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}}^\frac{\xi}{2} +\Parentheses*{\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}}^\frac{\xi}{p} }\\
				&\le \varepsilon^\xi + (t-s)^\frac{\xi}{2}\varepsilon^\frac{\xi}{2} + {(t-s)^\frac{\xi}{p}}\varepsilon^\frac{\xi(p-1)}{p}.
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		For the second term, apply {Jensen's} inequality to see
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:j11 bound approx poisson}
			\begin{aligned}
				\left( e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon {\le} T+1} \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!} \left(\varepsilon(n+1) \right)^{-\beta \frac{p}{p-1}} \right)^\frac{p-1}{p} &\le \left(e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon}\sum_{n\varepsilon {\le} T+1} \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!} \left(\varepsilon(n+1) \right)^{-1}\right)^{\beta }\\
				& \le \left(e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n = 0}^\infty \frac{\left(\frac{t-s}{\varepsilon} \right)^n}{n!} \frac{1}{\varepsilon(n+1)}\right)^{\beta}\\
				&= \left(e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n = 0}^\infty  \frac{1}{t-s}\frac{\left( \frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\right)^{\beta}\\
				&= \left(\frac{1}{t-s} e^{-(t-s)/\varepsilon} \sum_{n = 1}^\infty \frac{\left( \frac{t-s}{\varepsilon}\right)^n}{n!}\right)^{\beta}\\
				&\le (t-s)^{-\beta}
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		Inserting \eqref{ineq:j11 bound approx poisson} and \eqref{ineq:j1 decomposition poisson moments} into \eqref{ineq:j1 decomposition approx poisson}, we see it holds uniformly in $T+1\ge t>s\ge 0$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ that
		\begin{align}\label{ineq:j1 final bound}
			J_{1,T}(t-s,\varepsilon) \lesssim \Parentheses*{\varepsilon^\xi + (t-s)^\frac{\xi}{2}\varepsilon^\frac{\xi}{2} + {(t-s)^\frac{\xi}{p}}\varepsilon^\frac{\xi(p-1)}{p}} (t-s)^{-\beta}.
		\end{align}
		Inserting this and \eqref{ineq:j2 decomposition poisson moments} into \eqref{ineq:j1j2 composition approx poisson}, we see
		\begin{align*}
			\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0} \sup_{t \in (0,T+1]\atop x \in {\mathbb X}} \int_0^tI_{2,T}(t-s,x,\varepsilon) \mathrm ds = 0.
		\end{align*}
		Collecting all terms, we see \eqref{lim:sup_cov} holds.
		
		
		For (3), {let $\beta$ be as in \eqref{cond:time_holder}.} By Lemma \ref{lem:hk longtime}, we see for each $T>0$, it holds uniformly in $t \in (0,T]$ that
		\begin{align}
			&\int_{\mathbb X} \left|r_t^\varepsilon({y,x}) - G_t({y,x}) \right| m(\mathrm dy)\\
			\lesssim& e^{-\frac{t}{\varepsilon}} \left( 1+\sum_{n \varepsilon \le T + 1}  \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!} \int_{\mathbb X}\left|G_{n\varepsilon}({y,x}) - G_t({y,x}) \right|m(\mathrm dy) +  2\sum_{n \varepsilon > T + 1}  \frac{(e^{\kappa \varepsilon}t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!}\right).
		\end{align}
		Let $\psi(\lambda) = (\lambda+1)\ln(\lambda+1) - \lambda$ and {$c_T = \min\left\{\frac12\psi\left(\frac{1}{2T}\right),1\right\}$}. By Bennett's inequality for Poisson random variables, it holds uniformly in sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$ and all $t \in (0,T]$ that 
		\begin{align}
			e^{-t/\varepsilon}\sum_{n\varepsilon > T+1} \frac{(e^{\kappa \varepsilon}t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!}
			&= {e^{(e^{\kappa\varepsilon}-1)t/\varepsilon}}e^{-e^{\kappa\varepsilon}t/\varepsilon}
			\sum_{n\varepsilon>T+1}\frac{(e^{\kappa\varepsilon}t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!}\\
			&\le {e^{(e^{\kappa\varepsilon}-1)t/\varepsilon}}
			\exp\left(-\frac{e^{\kappa\varepsilon}t}{\varepsilon}
			\psi\left({\frac{T+1}{t}e^{-\kappa\varepsilon}-1}\right)\right)\\
			&\lesssim {\exp\left(-c_T\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)}.
		\end{align}
		Here we used that $(e^{\kappa\varepsilon}-1)/\varepsilon$ is bounded for $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$, and, for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, $e^{\kappa\varepsilon}\ge\frac12$ and $\frac{T+1}{t}e^{-\kappa\varepsilon}-1\ge\frac1{2T}$.
		This gives the required exponential term in \eqref{ineq:p_approx_m}.

		Let $\xi:=\xi_1\wedge1$, choose $k\in\mathbb{N}$ sufficiently large, and set $p:=2k/\xi$ and $q:=p/(p-1)$ so that $q\beta<1$. By \eqref{ineq:time_holder_long_time} and H\"{o}lder's inequality, the following inequality holds uniformly in $(t,x) \in [0,T]\times {\mathbb X}$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$:
		\begin{align}
			&e^{-t/\varepsilon}\sum_{n\varepsilon\le T + 1}  \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!} \int_{\mathbb X}\left|G_{n\varepsilon}({y,x}) - G_t({y,x}) \right|m(\mathrm dy)\\
			\lesssim&  e^{-t/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon\le T+1} \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!}\left(\frac{1}{(n\varepsilon)^\beta} + \frac{1}{t^\beta} \right)  |n\varepsilon - t|^{\xi}\\
			\le& \eta_\varepsilon(t) \left( t^{-\beta} + I(t) \right)
		\end{align}
		where {$\eta_\varepsilon (t):= \left(e^{-t/\varepsilon} \sum_{n\varepsilon\le T+1} \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!} |n\varepsilon - t|^{p\xi} \right)^\frac{1}{p}$} and
		\begin{align}
			I(t) &:=\left( e^{-t/\varepsilon}\sum_{n\varepsilon\le T+1} \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!} \frac{1}{\left(n\varepsilon \right)^{q\beta}}\right)^\frac{1}{q}\\
			&\le \left( e^{-t/\varepsilon}\sum_{n = 1}^\infty \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!} \frac{1}{n\varepsilon}\right)^\beta\\
			&\le \frac{2}{t^\beta} \left(e^{-t/\varepsilon} \sum_{n = 0}^\infty \frac{(t/\varepsilon)^n}{n!} \right)^\beta\\
			&= \frac{2}{t^\beta},
		\end{align}
		where we used Jensen's inequality in the second line. Combining all the above, we see uniformly in $(t,x) \in {(0,T]} \times {\mathbb X}$ and sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$, it holds that
		\begin{align}
			\int_{\mathbb X} \left|r_t^\varepsilon(y,x) - G_t(y,x) \right| {m(\mathrm dy)} \lesssim e^{-c_T \frac{t}{\varepsilon}} + \eta_\varepsilon(t) t^{-\beta}.
		\end{align}
		{Finally, by the even-moment case of Lemma \ref{lem:poisson moment bounds}, it holds uniformly in $t \in (0,T]$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ that}
		\begin{align*}
			\eta_\varepsilon(t)
			&\le {\varepsilon^\xi{\mathbb E}\left[\Absolute*{N_\varepsilon(t)-\frac{t}{\varepsilon}}^{p\xi}\right]^\frac1p}\\
			&\lesssim {\varepsilon^{\xi-\frac1p}t^{\frac1p}
			+\varepsilon^{\frac\xi2}t^{\frac\xi2}}\\
			&\lesssim {\varepsilon^{\xi-\frac1p}+\varepsilon^{\frac\xi2}}
			=: \gamma_\varepsilon,
		\end{align*}
		Since $p\xi\ge2$, {$\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\gamma_\varepsilon=0$}, which proves the statement.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- lemma-31: body_md -->
\label{lem:approx of identity of heat kernel}
		Denote for $x,y \in {\mathbb X}$ that $d_1(x,y):= d(x,y) \wedge 1$. Then for any $\xi >0$ and $\lambda >0$, 
		\begin{align}
			\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0} \sup_{ x \in {\mathbb X}} h(x)^{-\lambda}\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x) d_1(x,y)^\xi m(\mathrm dy) = 0.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-31 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
Fix $\xi,\lambda>0$ and restrict $\varepsilon$ to $(0,1]$. By \eqref{cond:exp_hk_lamda} with $\lambda=0$,
		\begin{align*}
			M:=\sup_{0<\varepsilon\le1}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}(P_\varepsilon^*1)(x)<\infty.
		\end{align*}
		Let $r\in(0,1)$. By Lemma \ref{lem:ref_grow} and compactness of closed bounded balls, choose a compact set $K\subset{\mathbb X}$ such that $h(x)^{-\lambda}\le r^\xi$ for $x\notin K$; if ${\mathbb X}$ is compact, take $K={\mathbb X}$. Choose $x_1,\ldots,x_N\in K$ with $K\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^N B_r(x_i)$, and set $f_i(y):=d_1(y,x_i)^\xi$. Each $f_i$ is bounded and continuous, so \eqref{cond:ctem continuous} gives
		\begin{align}\label{eq:approx identity of hk uniform convergence K}
			E_\varepsilon:=\max_{1\le i\le N}\sup_{x\in K}\Absolute*{(P_\varepsilon^*f_i)(x)-f_i(x)}\longrightarrow0
			\qquad\text{as }\varepsilon\downarrow0.
		\end{align}
		For $x\in K$, choose $i$ with $d(x,x_i)<r$. The triangle inequality for $d_1$ gives, with $C:=\max\{1,2^{\xi-1}\}$,
		\begin{align*}
			\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)d_1(y,x)^\xi m(\mathrm dy)
			&\le C\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)\Parentheses*{d_1(y,x_i)^\xi+d_1(x_i,x)^\xi}m(\mathrm dy)\\
			&\le C\Parentheses*{(P_\varepsilon^*f_i)(x)+r^\xi(P_\varepsilon^*1)(x)}\\
			&\le C\Parentheses*{E_\varepsilon+(1+M)r^\xi}.
		\end{align*}
		For $x\notin K$,
		\begin{align*}
			h(x)^{-\lambda}\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)d_1(y,x)^\xi m(\mathrm dy)
			\le h(x)^{-\lambda}(P_\varepsilon^*1)(x)
			\le Mh(x)^{-\lambda}\le Mr^\xi.
		\end{align*}
		Combining the estimates above and using $h\ge1$, we obtain
		\begin{align*}
			\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)d_1(y,x)^\xi m(\mathrm dy)
			\le C(1+M)r^\xi.
		\end{align*}
		Letting $r\downarrow0$ proves the assertion.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
\emph{Step 1.} We first construct the regularized
		coefficients and verify the coefficient properties required in
		\eqref{eq:FineNConditions}.
		After redefining $b$ and $\sigma$ to be zero on the common exceptional null set, if necessary, we may assume that the standing coefficient conditions hold for every $\omega\in\Omega$.
		Let us define for each $(t,\omega,u,x) \in \mathbb{R}_+\times \Omega \times \mathbb{R}\times {\mathbb X}$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ that
		\begin{gather}
			b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u):= \int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x) b(\omega,t,y,u) m(\mathrm dy),\\
			\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u):= \int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x) \sigma(\omega,t,y,u) m(\mathrm dy).
		\end{gather}
		Since $G_\varepsilon$ is deterministic and non-negative, both
		functions are predictable, and  \eqref{ineq:non_negativity_coef_bound_at_zero} gives  
		\begin{align}
			b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,0)&\geq0,
			&\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,0)&=0.
		\end{align}
		Moreover, by \eqref{eq:LiG}, \eqref{ineq:g_lip},
		\eqref{ineq:non_negativity_coef_bound_at_zero}, and
		\eqref{cond:exp_hk_lamda} with $\lambda=0$, for each $T>0$, uniformly
		in $\omega\in\Omega$, $(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb X}$,
		$\varepsilon\in(0,1]$, and $u,v\in\mathbb{R}$,
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:approx_coef_uniform_bounds}
			\begin{aligned}
			\Absolute*{b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u)}\lesssim1&+|u|,
			\qquad
			\Absolute*{\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u)}\lesssim|u|,\\
			\Absolute*{b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u)-b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,v)}
			&+\Absolute*{\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u)-\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,v)}
			\lesssim|u-v|.
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		In addition, by
		\eqref{cond:time_holder} when $d(x,y)\le1$, and by \eqref{ineq:approx_coef_uniform_bounds} together with $\varepsilon^{-\beta}d(x,y)^{\xi_2}\ge1$ when $d(x,y)>1$, it holds uniformly in $\omega\in\Omega$, $(t,x,y)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb X}^2$, $u\in\mathbb{R}$, and
		$\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ that 
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:approx_coef_joint_holder}
			\begin{aligned}
				\Absolute*{b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u)-b_\varepsilon(\omega,t,y,u)}
				&\lesssim \varepsilon^{-\beta}d(x,y)^{\xi_2}(1+|u|),\\
				\Absolute*{\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,x,u)-\sigma_\varepsilon(\omega,t,y,u)}
				&\lesssim \varepsilon^{-\beta}d(x,y)^{\xi_2}|u|,
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		where $\xi_2 \in (0,1]$ is as in \eqref{cond:time_holder}.
		Thus \eqref{ineq:non_negative_approx_coef_ineq} holds with $\eta=\xi_2$.
		Together with the Lipschitz bounds in $u$, the spatial estimates also
		imply \eqref{eq:con} for $b_\varepsilon$ and $\sigma_\varepsilon$.
		Consequently, all coefficient properties required in
		\eqref{eq:FineNConditions} hold at the $\varepsilon$-level. Therefore,
		for each $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ and
		$f\in\mathcal C_{\mathbb X}^{\mathrm b}\subset\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tem}}_{{\mathbb X},h}$, there exists a
		unique solution $u_\varepsilon$ to
		\begin{align}
			u_\varepsilon(t,x) = P^*_tf(x)&+ \int_0^t \mathrm ds \int_{\mathbb X} G(t-s,y,x) b_\varepsilon(s,y,u_\varepsilon(s,y)) m(\mathrm dy)\\
			&+\iint_0^t G(t-s,y,x) \sigma_\varepsilon(s,y,u_\varepsilon(s,y)) W(\mathrm dy,\mathrm ds).
		\end{align}
		\begin{samepage}
		\emph{Step 2.} It remains to prove
		\eqref{eq:Approximation}. Let $u$ be the solution to \eqref{eq:spde}, fix arbitrary $T>0$ and
		$\lambda\in(0,1]$, and set for $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ and $(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb X}$ that
		\begin{align}
			D_\varepsilon(t,x)&:=u_\varepsilon(t,x)-u(t,x),&
			V_\varepsilon(t)&:=\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			{\mathbb E}\big[|D_\varepsilon(t,x)|^2\big].
		\end{align}
		\end{samepage}
		To separate the coefficient differences into the solution error, a
		spatial-averaging error, and an approximation-of-the-identity residual,
		write
		\[
			B_s(x):=b(s,x,u(s,x)),\qquad
			\Sigma_s(x):=\sigma(s,x,u(s,x)),
		\]
		and set
		\[
			Q_\varepsilon(s,x):=\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)
			\Norm*{u(s,y)-u(s,x)}_{L^2_\Omega}m(\mathrm dy).
		\]
		By the definition of $b_\varepsilon$, Minkowski's inequality and \eqref{ineq:g_lip}, it
		holds uniformly in $(s,x)\in(0,T]\times{\mathbb X}$ and
		$\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ that
		\begin{align*}
			\Norm*{b_\varepsilon(s,x,u_\varepsilon(s,x))-B_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			&\leq
			\Norm*{b_\varepsilon(s,x,u_\varepsilon(s,x))
			-b_\varepsilon(s,x,u(s,x))}_{L^2_\Omega}\\
			&\quad+\Norm*{\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)
			\left[b(s,y,u(s,x))-b(s,y,u(s,y))\right]m(\mathrm dy)}_{L^2_\Omega}\\
			&\quad+\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}\\
			&{\overset{\eqref{ineq:g_lip}}{\lesssim}}
			(P_\varepsilon^*\mathbf1)(x)
			\Norm*{D_\varepsilon(s,x)}_{L^2_\Omega}+Q_\varepsilon(s,x)
			+\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}\\
			&{\overset{\eqref{cond:exp_hk_lamda}}{\lesssim}}
			\Norm*{D_\varepsilon(s,x)}_{L^2_\Omega}+Q_\varepsilon(s,x)
			+\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}.
		\end{align*}
		Similarly, it holds uniformly in $(s,x)\in(0,T]\times{\mathbb X}$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ that
		\begin{align*}
			\Norm*{\sigma_\varepsilon(s,x,u_\varepsilon(s,x))-\Sigma_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			{\lesssim}
			\Norm*{D_\varepsilon(s,x)}_{L^2_\Omega}+Q_\varepsilon(s,x)
			+\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}.
		\end{align*}
		Hence, it holds uniformly in $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ and $t\in[0,T]$ that 
		\begin{align*}
			&\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{\int_0^t\!\int_{\mathbb X} G(t-s,y,x)
			\left[b_\varepsilon(s,y,u_\varepsilon(s,y))-B_s(y)\right]
			m(\mathrm dy)\mathrm ds}_{L^2_\Omega}^2\\
			{\overset{\eqref{ineq:hk_m long time}}{\lesssim}}&
			\left(\int_0^t\left[
			V_\varepsilon(s)^{1/2}
			+\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}Q_\varepsilon(s,y)
			+\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(y)-B_s(y)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			\right]\mathrm ds\right)^2\\
			{\lesssim}&
			\int_0^tV_\varepsilon(s)\,\mathrm ds
			+\left(\int_0^t\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}
			Q_\varepsilon(s,y)\,\mathrm ds\right)^2+
			\left(\int_0^t\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(y)-B_s(y)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			\,\mathrm ds\right)^2.
		\end{align*}
		In addition, it holds uniformly in $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ and $t\in[0,T]$ that
		\begin{align*}
			&\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{\iint_0^t G(t-s,y,x)
			\left[\sigma_\varepsilon(s,y,u_\varepsilon(s,y))-\Sigma_s(y)\right]
			W(\mathrm ds,\mathrm dy)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2\\
			{\overset{\eqref{ineq:mtg+mink+cs}}{\lesssim}}&
			\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}\int_0^t
			\Norm*{G(t-s,\cdot,x)
			\Norm*{\sigma_\varepsilon(s,\cdot,u_\varepsilon(s,\cdot))
			-\Sigma_s(\cdot)}_{L^2_\Omega}}_{\mathcal H_K}^2\,\mathrm ds\\
			{\overset{\eqref{ineq:hk_k long time}}{\lesssim}}&
			\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}\left[
			V_\varepsilon(s)^{1/2}
			+\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}Q_\varepsilon(s,y)
			+\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(y)-\Sigma_s(y)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			\right]^2\mathrm ds\\
			{\lesssim}&
			\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}V_\varepsilon(s)\,\mathrm ds
			+\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\left[\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-\lambda}Q_\varepsilon(s,y)\right]^2
			\mathrm ds\\
			&\qquad+
			\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}\sup_{y\in{\mathbb X}}h(y)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(y)-\Sigma_s(y)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2
			\,\mathrm ds.
		\end{align*}
		Therefore, it holds uniformly in $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ and $t\in[0,T]$ that
		\begin{align}\label{ineq:approximation_master}
			V_\varepsilon(t)\lesssim{}&
			\int_0^t\bigl(1+(t-s)^{-\alpha}\bigr)
			V_\varepsilon(s)\,\mathrm ds
			+\left(\int_0^t\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			Q_\varepsilon(s,x)\,\mathrm ds\right)^2\nonumber\\
			&+\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\left[\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			Q_\varepsilon(s,x)\right]^2\mathrm ds\nonumber\\
			&+\left(\int_0^t\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			\,\mathrm ds\right)^2\nonumber\\
			&+\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2
			\,\mathrm ds.
		\end{align}
		\emph{Step 3.} We first control the spatial-averaging term
		$Q_\varepsilon$, which gives the second and third terms on the
		right-hand side of \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}. Similar to the
		$J_7$ term in the proof of
		Proposition~\ref{thm:non_negtive}, we see \eqref{ineq:u_bounded_spatial_increment} holds. Now, let
		$\xi'\in(0,1)$ be as in \eqref{ineq:u_bounded_spatial_increment}, and set
		\[
			q_\varepsilon:=\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			\int_{\mathbb X} G_\varepsilon(y,x)d_1(x,y)^{\xi'}m(\mathrm dy),
		\]
		where $d_1$ is as in Lemma~\ref{lem:approx of identity of heat kernel}. Hence, $\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0 }q_\varepsilon=0$.
		Thus, by \eqref{ineq:u_bounded_spatial_increment} and
		\eqref{cond:exp_hk_lamda}, it holds uniformly in $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ and $s\in(0,T]$ that
		\begin{align}\label{ineq:approximation_Qepsilon}
			\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}Q_\varepsilon(s,x)
			\lesssim 1\wedge\big(q_\varepsilon s^{-\beta}\big),
		\end{align}
		where $\beta \in [0,1)$ is as in \eqref{ineq:u_bounded_spatial_increment}. For the drift term, Lemma~\ref{lem:approx of identity of heat kernel} and dominated convergence give
		\begin{align}\label{ineq:approximation_2nd_term}
			\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0 }\int_0^T\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			Q_\varepsilon(s,x)\,\mathrm ds  =0.
		\end{align}
		\begin{samepage}
		For the third term on the right hand side of \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}, we may split the time integral to see that it holds uniformly in $\delta \in (0,T]$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$ that
		\begin{align*}
			\sup_{t\leq T}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\left[\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			Q_\varepsilon(s,x)\right]^2\mathrm ds
			&\overset{\eqref{ineq:approximation_Qepsilon}}{\lesssim} \sup_{t \le T} \int_0^t (t-s)^{-\alpha} \Parentheses*{1\wedge \Parentheses*{q_\varepsilon s^{-\beta}}}^2\mathrm ds\\
			&\lesssim
			\sup_{t\leq T}\int_0^{t\wedge\delta}(t-s)^{-\alpha}\mathrm ds
			+q_\varepsilon^2\delta^{-2\beta}
			\sup_{t\leq T}\int_{t\wedge\delta}^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}\mathrm ds\\
			&\lesssim \delta^{1-\alpha}
			+q_\varepsilon^2\delta^{-2\beta}.
		\end{align*}
		\end{samepage}
		Letting first $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and then $\delta\downarrow0$ yields
		\begin{align}\label{ineq:approximation_3rd_term}
			\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\sup_{t\leq T}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\left[\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			Q_\varepsilon(s,x)\right]^2\mathrm ds=0.
		\end{align}
		\emph{Step 4.} It remains to control the last two terms in \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}. Fix $p>2$ and $R>\|f\|_{(\lambda/2)}$, and set
		\[
			A_R:=\left\{\sup_{0\leq r\leq T}
			\Norm*{u(r,\cdot)}_{(\lambda/2)}\leq R\right\}.
		\]
		By \eqref{eq:LiG},\eqref{ineq:g_lip},\eqref{eq:con} and the fact that $u\in\mathcal C_{\mathbb{R}_+}\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tem}}_{{\mathbb X},h}$, we see  $B_s,\Sigma_s\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tem}}_{{\mathbb X},h}$ for every $s\in[0,T]$ almost surely. Hence, by \eqref{cond:ctem continuous}, for every
		$s\in[0,T]$, it holds almost surely that
		\begin{equation}\label{eq:approximation_good_terms}
			\begin{aligned}
				\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}\mathbf1_{A_R}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
				\left(\left|P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)\right|
				+\left|P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)\right|\right)
				=0.
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		By the dominated convergence theorem, we see it holds for every fixed $R$ that 
		\begin{align*}
			&\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}\int_0^T\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			\Norm*{\mathbf1_{A_R}\big(P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)\big)}_{L^2_\Omega}
			\,\mathrm ds=0,\\
			&\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}\int_0^T\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{\mathbf1_{A_R}\big(P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)\big)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2
			\,\mathrm ds=0.
		\end{align*}
		By H\"older's inequality, Corollary~\ref{cor:moment},
		\eqref{eq:LiG} and \eqref{ineq:hk_m long time}, it holds uniformly in $R$ that
		\begin{equation}\label{ineq:approximation_bad_term}
			\begin{aligned}
				&\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sup_{s\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}
				h(x)^{-\lambda}
				\Norm*{\mathbf1_{A_R^c}\big(P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)\big)}_{L^2_\Omega}\lesssim
				\mathbb P(A_R^c)^{1/2-1/p}\\
				&\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sup_{s\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}
				h(x)^{-\lambda}
				\Norm*{\mathbf1_{A_R^c}\big(P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)\big)}_{L^2_\Omega}\lesssim
				\mathbb P(A_R^c)^{1/2-1/p}.
			\end{aligned}
		\end{equation}
		Since $u\in\mathcal C_{\mathbb{R}_+}\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tem}}_{{\mathbb X},h}$,
		$\mathbb P(A_R^c)\to0$ as $R\to\infty$. Thus, letting first
		$\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and then $R\to\infty$, we obtain that 
		\begin{align}\label{eq:approximation_4th_term}
			\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0 }\int_0^T\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*B_s(x)-B_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}\,\mathrm ds
			=0.
		\end{align}
		This controls the fourth term in \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}. Since $\alpha \in (0,1)$, we see by \eqref{eq:approximation_good_terms} and dominated convergence that,
		\begin{align*}
			\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}&\sup_{t\leq T}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{\mathbf1_{A_R}\big(P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)\big)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2\,\mathrm ds=0.
		\end{align*}
		Furthermore, it holds uniformly in $R$ that 
		\begin{align*}
			&\sup_{t\leq T}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{\mathbf1_{A_R^c}\big(P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)\big)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2\,\mathrm ds
			\overset{\eqref{ineq:approximation_bad_term}}{\lesssim}\mathbb P(A_R^c)^{1-2/p}.
		\end{align*}
		Letting first $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and then $R\to\infty$ gives
		\begin{align}\label{eq:apprximation_5th_term}
			\lim_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}\sup_{t\leq T}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-\alpha}
			\sup_{x\in{\mathbb X}}h(x)^{-2\lambda}
			\Norm*{P_\varepsilon^*\Sigma_s(x)-\Sigma_s(x)}_{L^2_\Omega}^2\,\mathrm ds
			=0,
		\end{align}
		which controls the last term in \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}. 

		\emph{Step 5.} We now return to \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}. By inserting \eqref{ineq:approximation_2nd_term}, \eqref{ineq:approximation_3rd_term}, \eqref{eq:approximation_4th_term} and \eqref{eq:apprximation_5th_term} into \eqref{ineq:approximation_master}, we see there exists $H_\varepsilon$ with $\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}H_\varepsilon=0$ such that the following inequality holds uniformly in $t\in[0,T]$ and sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$:
		\[
			V_\varepsilon(t)\lesssim \int_0^t
			\bigl(1+(t-s)^{-\alpha}\bigr)V_\varepsilon(s)\,\mathrm ds+H_\varepsilon.
		\]
		We see by Corollary~\ref{cor:moment}, applied to $u$ and to $u_\varepsilon$ (the latter with $(b,\sigma)$ replaced by $(b_\varepsilon,\sigma_\varepsilon)$, see Step~1), that $\sup_{t\in[0,T]}V_\varepsilon(t)<\infty$. Hence we may use Gronwall's lemma to see that 
		\[
			\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}V_\varepsilon(t)=0,
		\]
		which is precisely the weighted
		$L^2_\Omega$ convergence in \eqref{eq:Approximation}.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
Let $\varepsilon >0$, then there are $N_\mathcal X = N_\mathcal  X(\varepsilon),\, N_\mathcal Y  = N_\mathcal Y (\varepsilon)\in \mathbb{N}$ with $N_\mathcal X \lesssim \varepsilon^{-\frac{\beta_\mathcal X}{\xi_\mathcal X}}$ and $N_\mathcal Y \lesssim \varepsilon^{-\frac{\beta_\mathcal Y}{\xi_\mathcal Y}}$ uniformly in $\varepsilon \in (0,1]$, so that 
		\begin{align}
			\mathcal X \subset \bigcup_{k = 1}^{N_\mathcal X} B_{\Parentheses*{\varepsilon/2}^{1/\xi_\mathcal X}}(x_k),\qquad \mathcal Y \subset \bigcup_{\ell = 1}^{N_\mathcal Y}B_{\Parentheses*{\varepsilon/2}^{1/\xi_\mathcal Y}}(y_\ell)
		\end{align}
		for some $\{x_k\}_{k = 1}^{N_\mathcal X} \subset \mathcal X$ and $\{y_\ell\}_{\ell = 1}^{N_\mathcal Y}\subset \mathcal Y$. Recall that 
		\begin{align}
				d_\mathcal S\Parentheses*{(x,y), (x',y')}:= d_\mathcal X\Parentheses*{x,x'}^{\xi_\mathcal X} + d_\mathcal Y\Parentheses*{y,y'}^{\xi_\mathcal Y}.
		\end{align}
		Denote the ball of radius $r>0$ in $\mathcal S$ under the metric $d_\mathcal S$ by $B_\mathcal S((x,y),r)$ for $(x,y) \in \mathcal S$. Then for $1 \le k  \le N_\mathcal X$ and $1 \le \ell \le N_\mathcal Y$, we have
		\begin{align}
			B_{\Parentheses*{\varepsilon/2}^\frac{1}{\xi_\mathcal X}}(x_k)\times B_{\Parentheses*{\varepsilon/2}^\frac{1}{\xi_\mathcal Y}}(y_\ell) \subset B_\mathcal S\Parentheses*{(x_k,y_\ell),\varepsilon}.
		\end{align}
		Indeed, suppose $(x,y)\in \mathcal S$ is a point in the set on the left hand side, then 
		\begin{align}
			d_\mathcal S\Parentheses*{\Parentheses*{x,y},\Parentheses*{x_k,y_\ell}} =d_\mathcal X\Parentheses*{x,x_k}^{\xi_\mathcal X} +d_\mathcal Y\Parentheses*{y,y_\ell}^{\xi_\mathcal Y}<\varepsilon.
		\end{align}
		On the other hand, we see 
		\begin{align}
			\mathcal S = \mathcal X \times \mathcal Y \subset \bigcup_{1 \le k \le N_\mathcal X \atop 1 \le \ell \le N_\mathcal Y} B_{\Parentheses*{\varepsilon/2}^\frac{1}{\xi_\mathcal X}}(x_k)\times B_{\Parentheses*{\varepsilon/2}^\frac{1}{\xi_\mathcal Y}}(y_\ell) \subset \bigcup_{1 \le k \le N_\mathcal X \atop 1 \le \ell \le N_\mathcal Y} B_\mathcal S\Parentheses*{(x_k,y_\ell),\varepsilon}.
		\end{align}
		Hence, uniformly in $\varepsilon\in (0,1]$, there exists $N_\mathcal S(\varepsilon) : =N_\mathcal X N_\mathcal Y \lesssim \varepsilon^{-\Parentheses*{\frac{\beta_\mathcal X}{\xi_\mathcal X} + \frac{\beta_\mathcal Y}{\xi_\mathcal Y}}}$ number of balls with radius $\varepsilon >0$ in $\Parentheses*{\mathcal S,d_\mathcal S}$  that cover $\mathcal S$, which is the desired result.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
<!-- proof-47: body_md -->
We first show $\ln(xy)-y(x-1) <0 $ for all $y >0$ and $x \ge 2$. Observe for each $x \ge 2$, the function $f_x(y) := \ln (xy) - y(x-1)$ is increasing for $0 < y \le \frac{1}{x-1}$ and decreasing when $y > \frac{1}{x-1}$.
		Hence
		\begin{gather*}
			\sup_{x \ge 2}\sup_{y>0} \left(\ln(xy) - y(x-1)\right) = \sup_{x \ge 2}\ln\left(\frac{x}{x-1} \right) - 1\le \ln(2) -1 < 0.
		\end{gather*}
		Therefore,
		$xy\exp(-xy+y)= \exp\left(\ln(xy) - y(x-1) \right)<1, \text{ for all } y>0,\, x \ge 2.$
		Multiply both sides by $\exp(-y)$ to get 
		\begin{gather*}
			xy \exp(-xy) < \exp(-y) ,\quad \text{ for all } y>0,\, x \ge 2.
		\end{gather*}
		This implies
		\begin{gather*}
			xy \exp\left( - \frac{y}{2} 2x\right) \le \exp \left( - \frac{y}{2}\right),\quad \text{ for all } y >0,x \ge 1.
		\end{gather*}
		Multiply both sides by $\frac{1}{xy}$ to see the desired inequality.
<!-- end proof-47 -->
